ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e265	Representatives of the Progress Party have made proposals on how to organise better working life for professionals. The rapporteur will praise the representatives for putting an important theme on the agenda, namely competence and security. For me, this is my first task as rapporteur, so I would like to thank an overall committee for a good cooperation and a very good tone in the work. The proposal proposes a series of proposals covering both daily rest places, refresher training for professional drivers and smooth driving training for foreign drivers. There is an overall committee that sees the importance of competitiveness in the industry and that drivers have good conditions. In its work on the proposal, the Committee also held a hearing on the matter, in which we received a number of good and important input. It is important that in the truck industry it is equal to treatment between Norwegian and non-resident carriers. The transport industry is important for business, and at the same time we know that the industry has the road as a job. It is therefore obvious that road standard and not least vehicle and driver are of satisfactory calibre. In this context, it must be said that at least the case rapporteur feels that work related to non-resident vehicles on Norwegian roads has recently received a strong focus. It's positive. After visits to inspection posts, at the latest during the Committee's visit to Hedmark last week, we see that the control work under security and competition conditions is a priority, and that is very reassuring. I will now move on to a slightly more specific right-hand position in the matter. Professional drivers are an important working group to ensure that businesses come into and out of goods and goods markets. The overarching thing for us is security for all who travel on the road, as well as an organized job for all who have their daily work on the road, and for professional drivers to be treated in a non-discriminatory way. With regard to the seven proposals that have been put forward, the Right will support two of these. Almost all professional drivers who drive a lorry over 3,5 tonnes and buses registered for more than 12 persons have to deal with driving and rest periods. These specify, inter alia: after a maximum driving time of 4,5 hours, a break of 45 minutes shall be taken. Today we have only nine approved resting places in the country. The Ministry points out in its letter of reply that 15-20 daily rest places are now planned until 2013. In the right, this work needs to be intensified and adapted to the needs of the industry. Good continuing training for professional drivers is important. The periodic training of professional drivers is currently required every five years under the EU Driver Directive. On the other hand, the Directive is open to interpretation of the way in which continuing training is to be provided, and this also reflects the costs of the Norwegian transport industry. According to Norwegian Professional Manager Allianse, the costs per driver in Norway are between 10000 and 18000 kroner. If you look at Denmark, the cost is 5000 kroner per driver. The right considers it necessary for the industry to investigate models in order to reduce the cost of continuing training. This is in line with what, for example: The NHO Transport pointed out during the hearing in the Committee. Right-wing believes it is important that we have a competitive transport industry in the country, and that is why it is an important issue The Progress Party travels. It is a matter of business and jobs and not least of safety on Norwegian roads. I would like to bring up the two minority proposals on this subject with the Progress Party on the Right.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e296	The representative proposal we are now debating is about a better everyday life for professionals. It's basically a commendable initiative and a good focus. We all travel the traffic every day, and that is the job of professionals. Therefore, improved road safety is an important part of the health, environmental and safety thinking of professionals. Therefore, it is a radical improvement in the daily lives of professionals that the number of traffic fatalities and the number of heavy traffic injuries are now historically low. But then it is difficult for us to understand that one of the proposals to improve the everyday lives of professionals is to remove the dot load system, a system that helps to save lives by following traffic rules and which, according to both Sintef and Safety Traffic, works well. It is the Progress Party that promotes this proposal in its proposal for a representative, and the most frightening thing, as we see it, is that the Progress Party writes that: ‘The point load system of the day causes small errors to have disproportionate consequences for drivers’. It scares us when driving for well over 70 km/h in a 60-zone is characterised as a ‘little mistake’ or when driving for more than 90 km/h in a 70-zone is a minor mistake. This is how I could go through the entire overview of the infringements that cause a dot load. What is produced from the Progress Party as a ‘small error’ is gross infringements of the Road Traffic Act and can be the difference between life and death. So how to remove the dot load system then better the everyday lives of professionals is a mystery to us. The Labour Party agrees with the focus on daily rest places and winter maintenance, and we show in this context the response of the Council of State. But we also want to keep focus on the fact that there will be more daily rest places, and I myself know that there will be a new daily rest place in the Nord-Trøndelag already in ours. We also point out that the State Road Service has announced an increase in winter preparedness, both for maintenance, for the closure of roads and for the use of other instruments that the State Road Service already has. The focus of the Committee's work on this proposal for a representative has been on non-resident trailers on Norwegian winter roads. From the Workers' Party, I would like to state that both Norwegian and foreign trailers are involved in accidents on Norwegian winter roads, and that measures that improve the safety of one group also improve the safety of the other. Even I've had the pleasure of being with the outdoor control group in the State Road Administration in the North Trøndelag one day on control in the Inner Namdalen, and it was a day of many useful recordings and a lot of learning. What emerges as the most important must be the following: firstly, it is about actively informing abroad about the Norwegian requirements. Because it is often not malicious that foreign drivers do not meet Norwegian requirements – it may be that you do not know. It is also the responsibility of the goods owners, or those who send or receive the goods in Norway. They have a responsibility for the transport they generate on Norwegian roads. We have to be more clear about that. Then it's about putting in place better and clearer requirements for tyre equipment, especially in winter. We hear that clearly from those who control – it doesn’t help if you meet today’s requirements, they’re not good enough. So if you travel beyond the border and are controlled and found in order, you can still stand in a hill further north of the country. And it's about looking at chain requirements. It may not be enough for the chain only to fit the wheels; it must actually be able to function. And then it's about further increasing control activities. Because it's already increased, but for our part we want to increase it further. These measures will increase safety on Norwegian roads. It will make both Norwegian and non-resident car combinations safer, and it will make industry more competitive, and at least it will not get worse because some people use poorer equipment. This applies to both Norwegian and foreign companies. Improving the daily lives of drivers – as the Labour Party sees it – is about improving road safety. Therefore, we do not want to remove the dot load scheme. This is about better technical equipment requirements for both Norwegian and non-resident wagon trains. But for us, a better everyday life for professionals is also about action against social dumping, focusing on collective agreements and pay and working conditions. This has not been mentioned by the Progress Party in one word in its proposal for a representative, and we must simply say that it does not surprise us.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e318	Our proposal to improve the daily lives of professional drivers is about looking at measures that can help improve the working day of the many thousands of professional drivers who make sure that Norway is kept going every day. They keep Norway going by driving food out to the shops, delivering goods to the construction industry and transporting goods to and from industry. Had it not been for this job that these boys and girls do, perhaps the slogan of Norway’s Lastebileier-Verbund – ‘Without the truck stopping Norway’ – would have been a reality. By sitting on the Transport Committee since 2005, I have met an incredible number of competent professional drivers. They have given me feedback as to what kind of everyday life they have behind the steering wheel of the car combinations, and how much of the everyday life they agree to drive on miserable roads. However, they are proud of their profession and take credit for the fact that the car should be both clean and in a proper condition. When these put their honor in the car and the way they behave on the road, I understand that they also expect the Stortinget and the State to take their responsibility and arrange for physical measures that allow drivers to comply with all the laws and regulations that we adopt. One of the really hopeless things is daily rest places. I note that in response to the Committee, the Council of State has promised that the Road Administration of the State will complete a 24-hour rest period plan early in 2012. Today we go into the third month of the year, and for the time being I haven't seen anything about the plan, but it may still be very early in the year for the Council of State. Those who are on the road and are stopped and fined for a breach of driving and rest periods expect that it will have to end waiting. They don't have time to wait, they need rest places now – not in five or ten years. So I really hope that State Council Kleppa puts in an extra gear to establish daily rest places at a great pace. A professional driver must comply with the Road Traffic Act and ensure that he keeps speed limits, relates to driving and rest periods and not least that the car is in a proper condition. But when it comes to the state, we've made sure to waive any responsibility. Anyway, the driver gets blamed. This cannot continue, and therefore a transport supervision should be established, which gives the right to penalise also the State Road Service, inter alia. standard road, daily rest places, etc. One of the major challenges we see on the road network is that it is increasingly rolling in foreign vehicle trains, which especially in the winter months have huge problems on Norwegian winter roads and Norwegian winter driving. This creates major problems with roads being closed because many of the foreign vehicle combinations lack the right type of tyres, chains, and the driver does not have experience in running on winter roads with such a bad standard. Unfortunately, we have received a number of examples of that. I know that you try to present it as if there are no more foreign drivers than Norwegian drivers who have problems, but a study among other things. in Valdres of 200 vehicle combinations showed that the whole 75 pst. of the vehicle combinations that had to be loaded were non-residents. The Progress Party therefore believes that this study, together with several others, clearly shows that we have major challenges with foreign vehicle trains – challenges that we have to deal with. It is both about the road safety of these drivers who are unable to drive during winter driving, and not least for all others who are travelling on the same roads and who are at risk of being met by a 40 or 50 tonnes wagon train, which is not equipped for traffic on Norwegian winter roads. That's why I'm very happy that the Council of State has clearly said that we need to increase the number of controls. With all this behind, it is a little disappointing that none of the other parties will support the proposal to ask the government to put forward concrete proposals to increase safety in winter travel with Norwegian and especially foreign trailers on Norwegian roads. But it's still not too late to turn around. I would therefore urge all the parties to vote in favour of the proposals of the Progress Party, and I would like to address the proposals that the Progress Party has made in this matter. Then to the representative Bratli. I record that the representative Bratli thinks it's okay that this is the only professional group that, if you make three mistakes – three mistakes – will lose the right to their livelihood. All other occupational groups are related to the Working Environment Act. It does this profession as long as they are in the terminal, as long as it is in the employment relationship, but as soon as they are on the road and make a mistake, they must not deal with the same work environment law as everyone else. The consequence is that this group loses its job, loses its livelihood and ends up in the social office. However, if a doctor makes one mistake, two mistakes or three mistakes, he usually only gets one package – and does not lose the job.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e398	It is important to contribute to a better everyday life for professionals even if these proposals do not flash. It has a very high impact on road safety and the everyday lives of professionals. We have a lot of winter in the north. This winter has so far been marked by a lot of ice and trackways, and it is worse than good winter roads. A study shows that the stopping distance for heavy-duty vehicles is significantly reduced by the number of multiple spikes. In June 2011, the Road Directorate sent a proposal to consult on the amendment of the Vehicle Regulation. The Directorate proposes to increase the permitted weight of certain heavy-duty vehicles and trailers. Furthermore, some adjustments to the permitted number of spikes are proposed. Today, 200 to 6 grams of spikes per deck are allowed on buses, but only 130 to 3 grams of spikes on heavy cars. Today's regulation is not satisfactory for the transport safety of a tank vehicle transporting dangerous goods and it is important that the number of spikes is also increased for heavy transport. Today, for example, petrol is being driven from Tromsø to Mehamn and Ifjord in Finnmark – a distance of 770 km is one way. On this journey, the transporters shall pass the mountain crossings of Sørkjosberg, Kvänangsbergellet, Baddereidet, Sennaland, Hatter and Ifjordfjellet. All these mountain crossings are hard to keep. Then it is important to be properly protected for the guide. Spike tyres are often set up against the environmental perspective. A load of dangerous goods in tanks is up to 47 000 litres, and in the event of an accident the environment will be very exposed, and in the worst case the whole environmental benefit is gone. 70 more spikes per deck appear to be a modest measure, but it will have a significant impact on the daily lives of professional drivers, at least northwards. Because when the accident is first out, every one of the spikes can count pretty hard.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e579	In this case, the size shall examine a proposal for a plan of progress in order to ensure an optional and dignified care for all older people. The draft document 8 consists of a total of 27 points. There is a broad consensus in the Committee that Norway should have a good and dignified, high-quality care for the elderly. It is also appropriate to clarify that the Stortinget has recently adopted a new law on municipal health and care services which states that the municipality should ensure that all residents of the municipality are provided with sound and necessary health and care services. This means that caregivers who need to be cared for by good luck are entitled to do so. Of course, it is very important to carry out supervision of municipal health services in order to ensure that this is realised. Of the 27 points, the proposal to transfer the financial responsibility for the care of the elderly from the municipalities to the state must be considered as the basis for the whole case. The proposal implies that when an elderly person in need of care is authorised at the institution or at home, the State shall be obliged to pay the service to the supplier, as the elder chooses. This is the introduction of a single-cost financing of the care of the elderly, which will undermine the entire model on which the care of the elderly in Norway is based. In the Norwegian model, the municipalities are responsible for the care of the elderly, and it is the municipalities, through framework funding, that have the freedom to adapt the care provision to the needs of the individual. Although the media may give the impression that things are bad in Norwegian care for the elderly — and there are many isolated cases that provide a basis for believing it — the model itself is not worth rocking by. Municipalities should be responsible for the care of the elderly because there is an unbureaucratic system that allows for the adaptation of care provision to the population of each municipality. The organisation of care for the elderly as proposed will stimulate the development of a bureaucracy and a diagnostic and reporting system that is both undesirable, heavy and expensive. It is worth recalling the experience of health businesses. Companies have a financial system to which the proposal can be compared. There's a bureaucracy built up every now and then, there's every reason to go closer to the seams. Therefore, a broad majority of the Committee does not support this proposal. I do not want to comment on all the points in this package. The Council of State has given detailed answers in the attached statement, but I will comment on some. Points 22 and 23 propose a State staffing standard for nursing homes, as regards both doctors and nurses, and also that the Health Directorate should be the approval authority for the crew plans. The majority of the Committee does not support this. The justification is that there is a different need for staffing in individual departments according to the needs of individuals around the care sector. A state norm can easily become a maximum standard that does not provide sufficient flexibility. The new Local Health Act states that individuals have the right to services adapted to individual needs. Then the need for manning will be different, also for patients with the same diagnosis. It is good for the patient that these assessments can be taken in the individual municipality and in the individual hospital. But let me stress that it is thoroughly documented that enough personnel with the right skills will be one of the most important challenges we face in the future. The vast majority of the elderly in nursing homes have complex disorders. Staff are required to do so. Older people face disease challenges other than those of the younger population, and the competences of elderly care must reflect these needs. Today, there are few demands for professional competence in hospitals and in the home service. In a 2009 report, SINTEF claims that it is mainly the working environment, working time and the low status of care services that prevent recruitment to the sector. We have to take this over. The report is based on interviews with help nurses. They describe a working situation in which they work either too much, too little or too much time. It is our job to ensure that more people take health training. The new Communication on Welfare Education, now being addressed in the United Kingdom, proposes more interventions that will make education more attractive and will help raise the professional level in the health and care sector. Among other things, a new model for the training of health and social workers in secondary education is proposed. It is proposed to introduce a new test system with the y-way – the professional path – where candidates with vocational education and training in health and social matters can be admitted to organised higher education and it is proposed to launch a test of health and social vocational education based on a secondary education other than a professional letter or professional competence in health and social matters. This will be the great challenge of Velferds-Norway for the next 20 years. In another point, 21 of the proposed document 8, it is proposed to earmark own resources for the implementation of the model from the municipality of Fredericia in Denmark – a model based on the strategy of the project «The longest possible in your life». The Committee emphasises that this model represents something very positive and is fully in line with what has been a priority in Norwegian elderly care for the past 10–15 years, namely strengthening home-based care. 80 pst. wish to live a self-employed life in owner-occupied dwellings or in an organised housing with common services when they grow old. Most people who receive help from the municipality live in their homes. It will therefore be very important to strengthen home services in the years to come. It involves continuing the fight against the stopwatching gypsy that is still present in many municipalities. The Hagerup Committee delivered last year’s NOU Innovation in Care, reviewing both the need for and possible solutions for increased use of technology in care services. The committee proposes, inter alia: a Telecommunications button 2015 for increased use of welfare technologies. It is very important for the government to follow up the NOU in the future. Care plan 2015 is the Government's action plan to ensure that society is prepared to meet the challenges of the future. The stable period of the next 10 to 15 years will be used to gradually expand the service provision to meet the rapid growth in the number of older people over 80 years expected in 2025. The care plan has four main fields of action: 12,000 new Annual Work Competencelift 2015 Demen plan 2015 construction of 12,000 lucky day care places This plan also includes the development of a new municipal role, strengthening the competence of municipalities, and greater prevention of municipalities. A very central element is that municipalities should have freedom of priority. This can be done by strengthening the municipalities resourcefully. This is not done through the proposal under consideration. The aim here is to deprive municipalities of this opportunity through the state takeover of funding, price financing and the construction of a diagnosis and reporting bureaucracy. I recommend the Committee's attitude.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e630	One of the most important and demanding welfare tasks for society in the coming years is to make sure that older people have a good and worthwhile life with good and adapted services for those who need it. Many of us will be dire and up to a long time in retirement, but it is also the case that many will become nurturing or will need various forms of help and assistance. The main idea of care for the elderly must be that the diversity and inequalities represented by the older population are the starting point for policy-making. That's what this is, among other things. is about in the care plan 2015 and in the dignity guarantee. This is why we have made it possible for people to stay longer in work and also combine different forms of work and retirement. That is why we need to think about housing, activities and experiences, and about care, care and practical assistance. We are not very satisfied with the care of the elderly all over the country, sail if we have to stress that there is a lot of good work done in a lot of municipalities. Therefore, I would like to point out that the Stortinget has adopted a new law on municipal health and care services that underlines even more the municipal responsibility. The necessary health and care services, practical assistance and other assistance shall be provided to those who need it. Health care in nursing homes or in nursing homes as well as early work on benefits in individual homes shall be offered to older people. Good supervision of municipal health and care services shall ensure that this is carried out in a good and sound manner. It is the individual municipality that should and must be responsible for delivering a good care offer to its citizens. The municipalities have the best overview of the people living there, and they know what needs exist. State grants shall enable each municipality to become a good welfare producer for its inhabitants. We therefore do not believe that state price financing, as proposed by the proposers, is the way to go. Many have set out the Danish Fredericia model and the project «As far as possible in their own lives» as an example for follow-up. It's great that there are good practices in the home and abroad that we can learn from. At the same time, I would like to stress that the cooperation reform is precisely proposing the kind of solutions that the Fredericia model prescribes – the proximity and quality of services and action at the best effective level. Examples already exist in many places, for example: in the municipality of Storfjord in Troms, where assistance and practical assistance were provided in the homes of the elderly at such an early stage that the municipality has had a long-term surplus in hospital places – which has been offered to neighbouring municipalities. Finally, I would like to strike a blow for the government-appointed Hagen Committee and the report ‘Innovation in care’, which was presented last summer. Several interesting ideas for future care for the elderly are outlined here, which were also discussed by the case-rapporteur – innovation in housing design, family, friends, voluntary sector and public actors in interaction to care for older people, proper use of communication and welfare technology. This may prove to be the right grips in the care of the elderly, combined with a deep understanding that we are living, playful, spiritual and cultural creatures throughout life – from cradle to grave...

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e702	The right-hand objective is to provide good quality care to all those who need it. Older people and caregivers are entitled to the necessary health care in the same way as everyone else, and that right should go as far as the need is concerned. A new law on municipal health and care services has been adopted which states that the municipality should ensure that all residents of the municipality are provided with adequate and necessary health and care services. But it does not help how much legislation we adopt here at Stortinget, unless the municipalities follow up their responsibilities. There is a lot of good in the care of the elderly. Thousands of skilled health workers make a great effort every day to provide those who need it with a good and worthwhile offer. Unfortunately, there are repeatedly large gaps in some care services, both through supervisory reports, through the stories of the elderly and their relatives, and through research and surveys in the media. Care in the final phase of life is one of the most demanding tasks in healthcare, challenging both professionals and politicians. Respect for human dignity is put to serious testing by politicians, health services and health professionals. Human dignity is especially vulnerable to being violated or threatened at the end of life. Let me give you an example of this. In recent days it has been revealed through the media that trauma, cancer and dying Sverre Østby from Eidsvoll were adopted against his and his family's will from safe and good surroundings in the hospital where he has had his home for the past two years, to another hospital. In addition to the feeling of having a dying man and father, the wife and daughter have had to fight an intense fight with the municipality in order for him to be there in the final phase of life. They themselves had to physically prevent the ambulance staff from taking him when they came to pick him up. Such a senseless treatment of a mortal man and his closest relative is insurgent and not to understand. It bears witness to a municipality where system and ruleriding are put in front of the individual's wishes and needs. There is a lack of respect and feeling – yes, simply pointless. The municipality has now, after the media releases, postponed the move for a few weeks, but the rapporteur from the Labour Party says that they stand firm on the decision to move him and that there are not enough arguments for Eastby to escape. I'm being upset about such a treatment. I do not doubt that Østby would also have good care and care at the other hospital, but that is not what this is all about. It is about taking into account the wishes and needs of individuals and relatives and showing respect and humanity at a vulnerable stage in life. It's outrageous that relatives have to go to the media to get their loved ones a good, safe and dignified end of life. I present this story as one of very many examples of violations of central patient and user rights and the Health and Welfare Act – in addition to the so-called dignity guarantee. In some areas, care for the elderly remains a long way off. The right-hand side supports several of the proposals that have been put forward, as indicated in the vote, inter alia. to make a change to the Law on Public Health in Municipalities, in order to introduce an offer to carry out preventive home visits to people over 75 years of age who wish to do so. We believe that this will be a good investment for the elderly and their safety and health. We also want to strengthen the clinical nutrition competence of the municipalities. There's a great need for that. Both those living in nursing homes and those living in their homes who live in their homes and those who live in their homes will enjoy it, not least it will have a health benefit. The right supports the proposal to introduce a certificate of good conduct for persons working with the elderly in the care sector, following a model from a requirement for a certificate of good conduct for persons working with children and persons with a mental retardation. In conclusion, the right does not support the proposal of the Progress Party to transfer the responsibility for the financing of the care of the elderly from the municipality to the state through the public interest. In order for care services to be as efficient as possible, municipalities must be guaranteed the freedom to meet these needs. By providing framework grants, municipalities may also make the necessary priorities in the overall provision of care.

Following the summary of the hearing opinions, the Ministry has found a basis for further consideration of these provisions. This was notified in the new Health and Care Services Act. It is difficult to establish a regulatory framework to suit all types of patient trajectory. The Ministry is therefore investigating several options. We will also consider the proposal to provide the patient with information on the time and place of treatment while providing them with an individual deadline. Nor is the regulatory framework for failure to comply with the deadline for necessary health care applied satisfactorily. Regional health establishments are responsible for providing patients in the region of residence with the necessary health care. This obligation also applies after the time limit laid down individually has been broken. If it appears that the healthcare provider cannot fulfil the right within this period, the undertaking is obliged to provide the patient with another treatment offer, public or private. It may be done by the health undertaking itself or by HELFO. HELFO is obliged to assist the patient in the fulfilment of the right from the time when the time limit is broken. This does not mean, however, that the responsibility of healthcare companies for providing treatment does not go away. I am not satisfied with how regional health companies have followed up this obligation. I therefore see that there is a need to clarify this responsibility even more. Health undertakings are also obliged to ensure that the patient is provided with the information necessary to safeguard his rights, and not least in order to carry out his treatment in the best possible way. The information shall be appropriate to the patient’s assumptions and shall be provided in a manner that the patient can understand. It does not believe that the patient is given a general rewriting of rights when granting an individual deadline for priority healthcare. If the undertaking is unable to obtain the necessary treatment by the deadline, it must inform the patient accordingly without delay. Furthermore, the patient must be informed of the consequences and of his rights in this regard. Good information is essential to enable patients to exercise their rights. In 2010, there were also questions about patients getting good enough information about patients’ rights from hospitals. I found it necessary to clarify this responsibility towards the regional health companies in a letter dated 20 September 2010. The responsibility for providing health care lies, as I said, with regional health businesses even after the individual deadline has been broken. That's still the way it has to be. HELFO is an offer to the patient that he can choose to use. The fact that the patient has an offer to receive assistance from HELFO does not relieve regional health companies of their responsibilities. It should also be the patient himself who decides whether to involve HELFO. I will follow up with the regional health companies that current legislation is to be complied with and will assess whether there is a need to change the legislation in order to make it more patient-friendly.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e1269	The issue we are about to deal with now is a proposal from the Progression Party on measures to ensure the rapid reconstruction of breasts after breast cancer operations. Breast cancer is the most common form of cancer among women in Norway, and about 2,800 are affected each year. About half of the women have to remove one or both breasts. This can lead to great stress; many experience fatigue, shoulder and arm pain, depression and long-term psychosocial problems. Decreased self-esteem, sleep problems and anxiety are not uncommon. For women, this is about identity, it's about setting a point for cancer and getting a better quality of life. The association for breast cancer surgery has said this: two breasts are not luxury, it is a pure necessity. Breast reconstruction is a very important factor in contributing to health, wellbeing and a good and full women's life. National Council for Quality and Priority in Health and Care considers that reconstruction should be part of the offer for breast cancer surgery in Norway. The Health Directorate has taken this into account in its work on national guidelines for breast cancer. It's an unacceptable long wait for reconstruction. The Health Directorate has informed that approx. 750 women need to be reconstructed annually. Many women have waited up to seven to eight years. An overall committee agrees that the waiting time is unacceptable and that it imposes a significant burden on women. There is too little capacity and limited access to public competences. Therefore, in the case of reconstruction, the offer needs to be significantly increased. Breathing instructions shall be included in the cancer action programme under the breast cancer guidelines. The disagreement between the government parties and the opposition lies in how this is to be done, the rights of women and the speed of reconstruction. The Advance Party, the Right Party and the Christian People's Party propose that breast reconstruction should be covered by priority healthcare under the Patient Rights Act, and that the maximum waiting time after a medical assessment should not exceed one year after the breasts or breasts have been removed. Government parties do not consider it appropriate to have a separate deadline, and there is reason to ask: not suitable for whom? At least that's for the women. They want to get a new breast within a year, they want to know what to do and not wait up to seven to eight years! The Government Parties refer to the Health Directorate Working Party to draw up the national guidelines to be completed on 23 March. It is precisely at this stage of work that the Storting can give clear information to the working group on the political leadership from which they are going to work. This is about three things: first, giving women who are operating for breast cancer, a right, priority health care with a maximum waiting time for reconstruction. Secondly, it is about building up sufficient capacity and competence in the public health sector. Thirdly, it is about taking away the long queues of women waiting for a new breast. We can give these rights to women now. Building up the necessary skills and capacity in the public sector will take a long time. The long queues can be taken away now if it is the political will of the government parties and the Minister for Health. But they're pushing it over to the health companies. We now need to do something about what we can – grant rights and get rid of the queues, in parallel with expanding the public offer. Experience shows that when patients get a right, it becomes a priority – otherwise it goes slowly! The Cancer Association, in a letter to the Minister of Health and Welfare, has shown that there is free capacity and great will in private clinics carrying out various types of breast reconstruction, and they will contribute to a real effort to reduce waiting time and waiting lists. But, of course, they depend on the regional health establishments to conclude the necessary contracts for the purchase of operations. Breast cancer-surgery women have waited long enough now. The Progress Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party therefore propose to the government to ensure that necessary agreements are concluded with private clinics and that this is explained in the context of the revised budget in 2012. However, the parties to the government will not support this proposal. They say that they do not impose any restrictions on the use of private operators, and that this is up to individual health professionals. But we know how it works in practice. The queues and waiting lists increase year by year. Several thousand stand in line, they are not given priority, and they refer to the lack of public offer. There are many women from all over the country in the gallery and outside the Stortinge today. What they have in common is that they are operated on for breast cancer, they lack a breast or two, and they have been on the waiting list for breast reconstruction for a long time. How much longer do they have to wait? It is not too late for the government parties to turn around, nor is it a shame to turn around and support the proposals of the opposition. Finally, I would like to address the proposals that have been put forward in the vote.

In Norway, it is natural to look at the petroleum industry that has been working on this systematically for very many years, with very good results. One has a similar opportunity to learn from aviation. It's not time to mention all of these 18 points in an article, but I'll still come up with some suggestions in each area. Balanced management is about management's attention being as high, and preferably greater, on quality and patient safety as on financial management. We believe it is right that the Storting should start with itself, because the Storting's discussion about health in connection with the state budget is very much influenced by the economic framework and not by results and security. Therefore, a modelled approach from the Privacy Policy Communication should establish a dedicated patient safety alert that takes into account both the nationwide supervision of the Health Authority and the work of patient representatives, thereby providing an annual monitoring message and a debate on patient safety status. So the Storting as the top body will also show that we care about this as much as the annual budgets. With regard to systems, there will be a need to put in place more national governance related to better internal control systems and an ISO certification requirement, which one has in other areas of society. It should just be lacking that one did not have this in the field of health where there is so much high-risk activity going on. When it comes to culture, it's really amazing that part of our society that is so heavily influenced by people with very high and long education, so little is a learning organization in the form of learning from mistakes that others have made, and being open to their own mistakes. Here, there's a need to do a lot of work to change this culture. The opposition believes that setting up a disaster commission that independently passes through the most serious incidents and analyses them will be one that can change culture.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e2131	The proposal under consideration is to provide a genuine guarantee of treatment for cancer patients. In the proposal, such a guarantee includes a legal right to treatment within 20 working days if there are no medical grounds for longer treatment. There is broad consensus in the Committee that patients affected by cancer should be treated promptly and that treatment should be of high quality. Most people today receive good and rapid help, and because of the high quality of the Norwegian healthcare system, most cancers also survive and become completely healthy. To have to wait a long time before getting a response to tests, to have to wait before making a diagnosis, and to have to wait, when you know that you need treatment, to start treatment, is too much – I would say all – to be a major psychological strain. Clearly, both patients and families become anxious and fearful when they do not receive prompt answers and prompt treatment. In the case of cancer survival, an improvement in results has been recorded in recent years for a number of cancer diagnoses, such as cervical cancer, breast cancer – which has been discussed earlier today – and colon cancer. In these areas, according to the OECD, Norway is the best in the Nordic region. But it is possible to bring down the waiting time even more. That is why hospitals are constantly working today to reduce waiting time. At the same time, the Council of State has called on health companies to provide 80 pst. cancer patients must undergo treatment within 20 working days, and in order to achieve this, hospitals must identify bottlenecks and improve organisation. Last summer, indicative lead-times were set in the cancer field, and this year the Health Directorate will follow up the time spent by hospitals. So this case is not about whether we want cancer patients to be treated quickly or not, but whether we believe that a treatment guarantee is the right tool to ensure that patients are treated more quickly. In its examination of this matter, the Committee draws attention in particular to the involvement of the Medical Association in the hearing of the Committee. The medical association believes that the provision of a general treatment guarantee for patient groups is problematic in principle. An individual assessment of the individual patient’s situation shall be carried out, and thus an individual assessment of the period of time for which it is appropriate to wait for the individual patient. This is in agreement with the majority of the Committee. A specific treatment guarantee alongside that provided by patients under the Patient Rights Act will not be an effective tool to improve the treatment provision of cancer patients. The majority of the Committee therefore proposes that the proposal be rejected.

As politicians, we have a responsibility to ensure both that health and care services receive the necessary growth and that the funds are distributed fairly. We need to combine increased financial frameworks and clear governance requirements with cooperation with the professional communities and patient organisations on good patient outcomes and the division of tasks between all parts of healthcare. I believe that it is not the juice that will provide shorter waiting time for cancer patients and increased survival. On the other hand, we must dry up to set ambitious targets for better cancer treatment. In the mission document of the regional health companies, I have therefore set a target of 80 pst. cancer patients shall be treated within 20 working days of receipt of the referral. The target is 80 pst. - Not 100. The reason is that for some cancer patients it is right to spend more than 20 working days investigating and clarifying what is the right treatment. Different hospitals will also take different time to reach the goal, and we know that it is more demanding for some patient groups. However, I am pleased to hear that the Cancer Association believes that there has been a lot of positive progress in the period after the target was set, with increased attention being paid to cancer treatment and the prioritisation of cancer in the mission documents. Hospitals need to identify bottlenecks and organise the supply differently. We measure the hospitals on this, and the results are published regularly, so that we can follow up the results. In 2011, we measured how fast the treatment started for breast cancer, colon cancer, and lung cancer. The figures show considerable differences between hospitals, but some hospitals manage to provide patients with rapid treatment. This year, I want to expand the measurements. Hospitals relate to a comprehensive legal and regulatory framework. They must organise themselves wisely and use resources as far as possible to provide all patients with good treatment. Professional requirements, guidelines and organisation shall contribute to the best possible results. I want to develop this work further, in close cooperation with the professional environments and patient organisations. There is no legal regulation that can replace systematic efforts to improve cancer treatment. Norwegian cancer doctors deliver world-class results, and we need to support them so that they can get even better.

In recent years, police certification requirements have been adopted in several laws, including: the Children's Protection Act, the Foreign Affairs Act, the Children's Court Act, the Training Act, the University and Higher Education Act, the Health Personnel Act, the Social Services Act and the Criminal Code. This means many closed doors to work for people who want and need a new chance. Large parts of the public sector are thus closed to people with experience skills on many of the challenges set by the same agencies to solve or prevent. On a general basis, I believe that there is a need to address the potential negative consequences of this, and a review of the seriousness of criminal offences should be carried out so that they should be covered by a police record. The introduction of such a police record into the Labour Market Act for people working in a trust relationship with people participating in the VTA will be an important complement to the prevention of abuses and infringements. ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e2437	In this proposition, the Government has presented proposals for amendments to the Labour Market Act requiring police clearance for employees in enterprises that offer the measure permanently organised work – VTA. VTA is a labour market measure in which development-impaired persons are included as a central target group. Developmentally disabled people are a vulnerable and vulnerable group and therefore need special protection. The requirement for a police attestation shall apply to ordinary staff. The requirement will include positions in which ordinary employees may, in the performance of their duties, enter into a trust or liability relationship in which abuse or adverse influence on persons with developmental disabilities may occur. The proposal implies that the employer has to assess the work area and the job portfolio for each job. Whether or not the action participants should also be subject to the requirement of a police attestation provides for a difficult balance between the need to protect some action participants and the need for the re-socialisation of other action participants. I would like to discuss this issue more closely in the context of the evaluation of the need for VTA action sites, which are now under way. The police record shall cover matters in which a person is intentional, accused, prosecuting or convicted of sexual crimes, intentional murder, serious crime of violence, and of criminal law and drug trafficking. The consequence of the observations made on the certificate shall initially be exclusion from work on the persons with developmental disabilities. The police record requirement shall not be retroactive, i.e. that the requirement will only apply to persons employed after the entry into force of the amendment. In this context, I refer you to the general reservation against giving legal provisions retroactive effect. I also refer to the fact that the employer, in the event of suspicion of criminal offences relating to an employee, has the opportunity to contact the police to obtain the necessary information. The obligation of professional secrecy of the police does not preclude the provision of information when necessary to prevent infringements or to prevent the conduct of activities in an irresponsible manner. Police attestations issued in some non-EEA countries could be of limited value. I therefore believe that there are good reasons for introducing minimum living requirements in Norway for workers outside the EEA area who are unable to provide a satisfactory police certificate. In my opinion, however, there is a clear advantage whether the requirement of living time is similar in all areas where there is a need for police certification. I therefore believe that this issue needs to be further assessed in cooperation with the other departments responsible for sectors facing similar challenges. The Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion has recently launched such harmonisation work. I stress that a good internal control system in the VTA companies will be the most important measure to prevent abuses against the developing population, even after the imposition of a police record. The introduction of police certification requirements does not in itself guarantee that abuses cannot occur, but is one of several necessary and important measures to prevent abuses of persons with developmental disabilities at the workplace. I am pleased that a joint committee supports the proposal to introduce police certification requirements in VTA companies and thus contributes to increased job security for one of the most vulnerable groups in the labour market.

Let's take an example: a student at a secondary school, who takes a vocational education, experiences systematic bullying and ends up at school because of this. At that time, it might have been natural for him to enter another school, and perhaps he would have been admitted to one of the vocational schools that many of our labour market companies have, in order to gain the skills to move on. But does it make sense for a student who goes from one school offer to another to have a special grant scheme, or a benefit scheme, when he does not have it in his ordinary school? I don't think that's right. With regard to those people who are older than high school and who have fallen out of work in various ways, who also enter the labour market enterprises and get a follow-up to move on: for these, I believe that this scheme is out of date in many ways, because we have redundancy payments. I think it's important in our welfare system that we clean up some of the concepts, that we coordinate some of the schemes and benefits. This will make the apparatus more effective, make it easier to deal with, and facilitate user assistance – precisely to lead them back to activity as quickly as possible, and to proper activity, so that they can get a step further. Then I have to say that I think it is a little strange that the representative of the Workers' Party spends so much time arguing against our proposal, especially given that the Council of State itself has said that she will start to review social security schemes and the level of benefits in connection with participation in measures. That's exactly what this proposal is about, but we're saying that we want this to happen in the current year. We believe it is important that we have proper progress on this matter, and that we have a guarantee that the Stortinget will be presented with such a review during 2012. I record that the majority and the majority parties, the government parties, say it is not so important when that matter comes, only it comes once in the future – it doesn't matter whether it will be in 2013, 2014 or 2025. I think it's important that we have a conscious drive in these matters, and I think that a co-ordinated growth could actually have supported such a proposal. Then we had cleaned up some of the terms here, cleaned up some of the arrangements – with which the Council of State is running – and we had to have delivered this in the course of the year. The government may do it anyway, but it would have been okay if the Storting could give such a clear signal when we want that review to be completed.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e2632	This issue is about removing the individual benefit, which proposers consider to be an incentive to leave secondary education in favour of participation in labour-oriented measures. Proposalers point out that the number of people leaving school has increased. It is common to define the rate of drop-out from three years of secondary education as not completing within five years. From this point of view, we see that the non-response has remained relatively stable on about 30 pst. in Norway since the mid-1990s. However, the level of non-response is too high and the government has therefore launched the New GIV initiative to increase implementation in higher education. The new GIV contains several projects, including the follow-up project, where I work closely with the Minister of Knowledge. Cooperation between the follow-up service and Nav should be strengthened to improve the follow-up of young people who have fallen away or are at risk of falling from secondary education. The goal achievement of the New GIV is closely followed. Recent figures also show positive developments in some areas. The proportion of students moving from Vg1 has increased by one percentage point from 2010 to 2011, corresponding to between 700 and 800 students. The individual benefit consists of basic benefits and any additional benefits to cover actual expenditure. Participants between the ages of 16 and 19 receive basic benefits at a low rate, NOK 227 per day, and participants over the age of 19 receive a high rate, NOK 313 per day. In addition, children’s allowances, child care allowances and out-of-home allowances are provided for those who have to live away from home. The basic performance shall ensure the benefit of life support to the action participants. Many of the youngest beneficiaries live at home, for which the benefit is more marked by an incentive to participate in measures. The proposalers focus on young people they believe are stimulated to quit school because of the individual benefit. However, leaving high school does not in itself give rise to individual benefits. The individual benefit is provided only in the course of participation in employment-oriented measures. The young person must first sign up to a Nav office and be registered as a jobseeker. Thereafter, the need for follow-up is assessed. One possible outcome is that a labour-oriented measure, including the right to individual benefits, is the correct follow-up. For young people who are far from work and education, the individual benefit may provide incentives to participate in interventions. We agree that continuing unemployment and passivity are not a good situation for young people. The individual benefit can prevent too many people from doing so. Participation and participation in secondary education need not be contradictory. An important element of the follow-up project in the New GIV is that the Nav and County Municipality, in cooperation, offer young people adapted combinations of labour-oriented actions and training. In this respect, the individual benefit may give young people who have fallen out of high school incentives to participate in a programme that can help them to complete their training. Proposalers are calling for an approved national target for professional subjects at a level of professional qualification. Today, we have this in that it is possible to take so-called basic competence through the apprenticeship system. Of course, if during or after the course of training the student wants to achieve full professional competence, it is open to it. Another way to achieve professional competence under the level of professional letters is the letter of practice, which is a pilot scheme that can be used by all states. We must always be open to assess whether existing schemes are working properly. However, I believe that the best way to combat high school drop-outs is through other measures such as those mentioned, and not by removing the individual benefit. As mentioned here by other speakers, there is no evidence of any direct causal relationship whatsoever between these two factors. Then I fully agree with the representatives who have said that we must constantly look at whether our arrangements function as good incentives for activity. That is why I have also stated that I am now going through the arrangements for young people to look at how they work, and I am going to get back to Stortinget in an appropriate way with the result of that review.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e2767	The present representative proposal contains three concrete proposals related to economic welfare or social assistance. Let me first highlight two important aspects of social assistance. Firstly, social assistance is meant to be the last safety net of society, and secondly, social assistance is intended to be temporary. The aim is to have the greatest possible financial autonomy. Then it is important that social assistance is followed up with activity and good advice. It is also important to have flexibility in instruments and closeness to users. That's why social assistance is a municipal rather than a state responsibility. The first proposal in this case is that municipalities should not be allowed to shorten the social assistance of their parents on the basis of income below the tax-free income limit of children under 18 years of age. The Committee is clear that children are not responsible for caring for their parents. The Committee is also concerned that children of social welfare beneficiaries, like other children, should enjoy their own income. When the majority chooses not to join in the proposal to introduce such a ban, it is in the light that we do not see any need for such a ban. We are aware that a new circular on the matter is being worked on, and we support the signals given by the Council of State in its reply letter to the Committee. It is also underlined in the majority's comments. The next proposal is to introduce a legal requirement for the obligation of activity for the individual social welfare beneficiary. It is a majority of the Committee which considers that a statutory obligation of activity, in the manner in which the proposal is addressed, is not appropriate. That is why one goes against the proposal. It's allowed to impose activity in today's law. This is used in varying degrees. The provisions of the law require municipalities to administer the law in order to prevent and combat poverty, and in very many cases it would be appropriate to combine this with activity. The Committee assumes that the local authorities follow this up, as required by law. When the municipalities in each case consider making demands for activity and provide a good social follow-up, it will help more people become economically self-employed. There are no good figures on the extent to which municipalities use this right to impose conditions on the payment of economic social assistance. There is a need for more knowledge of the use of activity. There is also a need for a more detailed assessment of the current social assistance framework. For this reason, the majority of the Committee, consisting of the government parties and the Christian People's Party, has chosen to ask the government to come back with an assessment of the extent to which social assistance can and should be combined with activity requirements and with proposals for how this can be done. The third point of the proposed document 8 is to make municipal social assistance state-owned and to introduce norms at almost the same level across the country. It is gratifying that it is only the Progress Party that supports such state-making of social assistance. Such an approach would weaken the ability of municipalities to carry out an overall effort to make citizens economically self-reliant. I believe that the proposal must be seen in the context of the traditional zeal of the Progress Party to state and red tape arrangements within the welfare state, including in other areas. To me, it is somewhat unclear what is contained in the concept of ‘standardised rates almost equally throughout the country’. It is that the proposal provides for geographical differentiation, and this is something new. No other benefits we have within our welfare state are geographically differentiated. I imagine a pretty big bureaucracy when someone replaces the local know-how and the local taste with a state welfare directorate. If you want to have your own directorate to assess these rates and the different cost-of-life conditions in the different municipalities – that is to speculate – it becomes a bureaucratic and poor system, which undermines what is the very foundation of social assistance, namely individual adaptation and follow-up. That is why one does not want to support that proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e2788	Social medicine, Steinar Westin, points out that the law on public health and the welfare systems that preceded it may have had a greater impact on public health than medical innovations by providing people with access to health services, good working conditions and improved hygiene and nutrition. The development of social arrangements that ensure a decent life for people has a long history in Norway. The Factory Supervision Act came in 1892, and it meant better working conditions for employees. The poverty code came in 1900. The sign correctly between worthy and unworthy needing. The unworthy, the proselytes, fantasies, beggars and people who had fallen into drunkenness were given their rights under the law of proselytism, not under the law of the poor. Fortunately, this is not the case today. Today's arrangements apply to all, and rights and obligations are largely predictable and well-practiced by the administration. Nevertheless, it is good for proposals to be made and for our welfare systems to be debated. We have to constantly consider whether we're on the right track, and whether we're doing the right things. ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e2861	The representative proposal contains three different social assistance proposals. What they have in common is that they will restrict the ability of municipalities to exercise discretion and make individual judgements in each case. I believe that is the wrong way to go. Today's social legislation obliges municipalities to adapt measures and services to the individual's situation and needs. The individual assessment and treatment are fundamental to social work. The economic benefit is one of several instruments. It shall, together with other services, help each person to overcome the difficulties that prevent him or her from caring for himself and his or her family. People who receive financial benefits often have problems other than purely economic ones. It is therefore important that the Nav Office, together with other public service providers, should be able to take account of the whole of the problem complex, and together with the problem in question, to find measures addressing the causes of the problems. The development of services in recent years has therefore been directed towards a greater — not less — degree of individualisation. The fact that children and young people in disadvantaged families are given good growth conditions in line with peers is fundamental to social services. This consideration shall also serve as a basis for assessing young people’s own income when a family is seeking social assistance. However, a scheme whereby municipalities can never take account of the fact that children under the age of 18 have their own income is not unproblematic and will include, among other things: cause demarcation problems. For example, how should this be done? a distinction is made between savings arising from the work of children and those arising from other sources, such as: heritage and gifts? I am also concerned that such an absolute rule can lead to some young people being forced to work more than they themselves want to contribute to the family's overall economy. The fact that children with parents receiving social assistance, in the same way as other children, will enjoy their own income will be further underlined in the new circular that is being prepared. I agree with the proposers that it is important to use active measures that can contribute to the self-sufficiency objective. I am concerned that the municipalities should use conditions of activity constructively. The law opens today to a decision on the obligation of activity as a condition for social benefits. This option is used when deemed appropriate. However, a legal requirement to have an activity obligation will have to be followed by a number of exceptions, relating to groups or individuals which, for various reasons, cannot or shall not be subject to an activity obligation. This applies, for example: persons receiving supplementary social benefits from social security and persons with heavy responsibilities for care. I want to ensure more information about the use of the activity requirement. I therefore support the majority of the Committee, all parties with the exception of the Right and the Progressive Party, which ‘will the government come back with an assessment of the extent to which social assistance can and should be combined with activity requirements and with proposals for how this can be done’. Proposalers want to state social assistance. I disagree. The crucial thing for users is that they receive good care and adequate timely support. Whether it is the municipality or the state that is responsible for the payments, hardly means anything to the individual. Proposalers also want social assistance that is more norm than it is at present. I believe that economic social assistance should still be a last safety net, based on the principle that this benefit is a subsidiary benefit and measured to meet an individual need. Any introduction of norm rates would be contrary to this principle.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e3079	The proposal from the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left proposes that the government submit proposals for improvements to the Youth Housing Saving Scheme, which must include an increase in the annual savings amount and ceiling of the scheme, and also an assessment of increasing the deduction from 20 pst. to 28 pst. The reasons given by the proposers are that Norway is among the countries in Europe where private savings are the lowest and that private savings need to be stimulated, especially among young people, in order to save on their first home. The proposers also point to an increase in housing prices over the last 10 to 15 years and an increase in equity requirements for the purchase of housing from 10 pst. to 15 pst. of the value of the dwelling, the entrance ticket for buying the owner-occupiers has increased sharply. I would like to further explain the majority's view. Let me make it clear at the outset that we support the intention of the proposers to increase private savings and agree that the BSU can help to increase the savings for self-employment among young people. However, with increasing housing prices and higher equity requirements for the purchase of owner-occupied dwellings, it is challenging for many to enter the housing market. This, together with the increasing debt of households, gives cause for concern. I would therefore like to stress that we believe that there is a need to consider more measures than the proposals made by the proposers to extend the BSU in order to help young people enter the housing market, not just to increase savings. The background for this is, among other things, with the same intention as the proposers, the government extended the BSU scheme already in 2009 so that the maximum annual savings amount increased from DKK 15 000 to NOK 20 000, the total savings amount increased from DKK 100 000 to NOK 150 000, while the tax deduction rate is 20 pst. Nevertheless, we see that in the relevant age group, i.e. between 17 and 33 years, it was only about 26 pst. used by the BSU scheme in 2009. Statistics from the SSB show a steady increase in the use of the BSU from 1993 onwards, and for 2010 it shows that there were 17 000 more people who used deductions for housing savings in 2010 than in 2009, approximately. 28 pst. So it's increased 2 pst. – with an increase in the deduction of close to 9 pst. For 2011, the value of the tax deduction in the BSU is estimated by the Ministry of Finance at about 770 million. kr. Despite an even increase in the use of BSU, there is a large majority who do not use the scheme. There may be many reasons for this, including: that young people of the age group in question are under education or for other reasons have no income and therefore have no savings nor are they able to use the tax deduction scheme. But it may also be that the BSU should be made even better known, so that more people use it. According to the letter from the Minister of Finance, a higher proportion of high-income young people use BSU than the proportion of low-income young people. Of those using the scheme, persons with higher income also have on average higher savings and tax deductions than persons with lower income. Therefore, there may also be a need to look at the current system sufficiently when young people with lower incomes. This issue does not concern the proposers in their proposal. It may therefore appear that the proposal to extend the BSU alone will not reach the large groups of young people who are going to enter the housing market. The Labour Party wants to facilitate the entry of young people into the housing market through the policies pursued, and there are several things that are already being done. The most important thing we do is to conduct a responsible economic policy that ensures jobs and a stable interest rate. This is the most important thing we can do for everyone, including young people looking for housing. Other instruments implemented by the government in recent years include an increased framework for various loan and grant schemes through the Husbank to meet, among others. home purchasers for the first time. This is e.g. start-up loans, which are a loan arrangement through the Husbanken for, inter alia, young people in the start-up phase who are struggling to establish themselves in the housing market or who are having difficulties living in their homes. It is the municipalities that manage the start-up loan. The House Bank shall assist each municipality with comprehensive solutions for those in need of assistance. Good use of start-up loans, housing grants and housing support can help more people to own their own homes or to establish a stable housing situation in a rental dwelling. Everyone should live well and safely. This is the objective of housing policy and one of the pillars of Norwegian welfare policy. It is therefore important to achieve this objective that we assess different instruments that we can politically contribute to. The BSU is one measure to support the desire for young people to buy their own housing, but it is not sufficient, if necessary, to extend this scheme, nor is it certain that it is the best way to give young people good opportunities to get into the housing market. The government has started work on a major communication on housing policy to be presented during 2012, in which the various instruments the government has to deal with young people who want to enter the housing market, including the BSU, will be assessed. The Committee proposes that the proposal be annexed to the Protocol.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-03-01.ana.ud2619e3626	The tax residence rules specify to which municipality personal taxpayers are to pay their tax. It is important that a person has rules to that effect, because they are essential for the distribution of tax revenue between municipalities and for predictability of the basis on which the municipalities operate. As long as taxes are payable to municipalities and counties, it is also necessary to have rules determining the municipality or county to which tax revenue is to be allocated. I think that's the basic thing in this case. One is considered to be resident in the municipality in which he has his home. Stay outside this home, e.g. as a result of work, the place of residence does not normally change. However, a taxpayer may be considered to have a tax residence at the new place of residence if the circumstances indicate that he has received his home there. Persons living with a spouse or a child are deemed to have their home where a spouse or a child is resident. Thus, the tax residence rules for spouses are based on the assumption that spouses live together on the same estate. I mean this is a natural rule, and there are enough people who agree with me on it. It also applies to most couples, as this is consistent with their desire to have a common home. But then, as others have been in the debate, it is possible for spouses to have different tax bulwarks. There may be several reasons for this, for example: – that someone has experienced – that someone has a dwelling obligation that needs to be fulfilled or other matters that are important in that context. In the rules on tax residence, there are no dispensations and the decision is based on an assessment of the facts according to objective and verifiable criteria, as it is called. For most people, these rules do not create any problems, because there is consistency between the solution on which the rules are based and on which most people perceive as natural for themselves. Most people will probably perceive today's tax residence rules as reasonable. As the representatives behind the proposal themselves point out, it is usually not a tax advantage for spouses not to have a tax common residence, but there are some other legislation that adds, for example, to the tax base. the choice of place of residence legal effects. Dissatisfaction with tax residence rules may also have its background in terms of the fact that the choice of residence confers rights under such rules. It may have an impact on commuting deductions, entitlement to hospitalization, or, as I have already mentioned, the fulfilment of the obligation to live on property. I have noticed that in the Committee there is a significant commitment to the principles that I have now had, and that tax residence rules should be based on objectively testable criteria. I therefore see, like the majority of the Committee, that there is no need to amend the legislation so that the tax residence of spouses can be chosen according to criteria other than those currently in force. But at the same time, I am also concerned that the legislation that is there, which allows for the possibility of having different tax residences, is practised in a manner that is considered reasonable and considered reasonable by those affected by the openings provided by the legislation.
